180	Reference *citations* are double-bracketed so the brackets render
181	— `[[N]]`, or `[[2]],[[3]]` for several (comma-separated, not
182	`[2,3]` or `[[2]][[3]]`). The `[N]:` definitions in a file's
183	`# References` section and inline `[text](url)` / `[text](#anchor)`
184	links stay single-bracketed. See
185	[Todo format](notes/README.md#todo-format) for details.
186	
187	### Markdown anchor links
188	
189	GitHub anchor algorithm: lowercase, strip non-alphanumeric
190	characters in place, map remaining spaces to hyphens 1-for-1. Do
191	**not** collapse adjacent whitespace — so `a + b` → `a--b` (spaces
192	on both sides of `+`), but `a: b` → `a-b` (only trailing space on
193	`:`). General markdown reference:
194	[markdownguide.org](https://www.markdownguide.org). GitHub
195	publishes no official spec for auto-generated anchors; the
196	de-facto reference implementation is
197	[github-slugger](https://github.com/Flet/github-slugger).
198	
199	### Headings and entries that record a commit
200	
201	A `chores-NN.md` `##` section header that records a specific
202	commit, the matching `todo.md > ## Done` entry, and any `[N]`
203	reference to that section all use **exactly that commit's
204	title** — `<type>: <desc> (<version>)`, the same string the
205	commit gets (see [Per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)).
206	E.g. the chores header `## refactor: port push to Context
207	(0.48.0-6)` and the Done line `- refactor: port push to Context
208	(0.48.0-6) [[3]]`. The `## Done` entry uses the close-out
209	commit's title.
210	
211	This does **not** apply to organizational headings (`## Todo`,
212	`## In Progress`, `# References`) or to design `###` subsections
213	inside a chores section — those are named for whatever fits.
214	Among the commit-recording ones, exact match is the strong
215	default (nothing absolute): a near-miss just makes it harder to
216	line a record up with its commit.
217	
218	A commit-recording header is provisional while the work is in
219	progress; the *last* edit before `vc-x1 push` syncs it — and the
220	`## Done` entry / `[N]` anchor for that commit — to the final
221	commit title. See [Markdown anchor links](#markdown-anchor-links)
222	for the slug algorithm; the pre-commit checklist catches a
223	dangling `#anchor`, and a future `vc-x1 validate-repo` should too
224	(and should verify the recorded title matches the commit).
225	
226	Existing sections and `## Done` entries (most of chores-01..09;
227	the pre-`0.48.1` `## Done` lines) predate this and keep their
228	free-form text; the convention applies going forward — `0.48.2`
229	converts the `0.48.1` section + its Done entry as the worked
230	example.
231	
232	### Chores section content — no edit list; git is the record
233	
234	A chores section is: a `Commits:` line (first line under the
235	header — see below), then [Prose form](#prose-form) (intro +
236	bullets) for what landed and why, and any `###` design
237	subsections. Bullets here are **conceptual** — design points,
238	structural notes — never a per-file edit list. That lives in the
239	commit message body, which is the source of truth for "what
240	changed mechanically" (immutable, `git show`-able, naturally
241	scoped to the commit). The chores section is the source of truth
242	for the design thinking; the two cross-link, neither restates the
243	other.
244	
245	When the intro starts wanting to explain a mechanism,
246	hypothesis, or wrinkle, don't inflate it — promote that to its
247	own `###` subsection inside the same `chores-NN.md`. If the
248	wrinkle is a live design concern (something that *should*
249	change, not just be recorded), also add a `notes/todo.md` item
250	with a `[N]` ref pointing at that subsection (todo→chores is the
251	normal ref direction).
252	
253	**Why:** a chores edit list and the commit body were specified
254	to be the same content in two places — and detail written twice
255	drifts. Git owns the mechanical record; chores owns the
256	narrative; `Commits:` links them.
257	
258	### Chores commit references
259	
260	The first line under a chores section header is a `Commits:`
261	line citing the git commit(s) that section records:
262	
263	```
264	## refactor: port push to Context (0.48.0-6)
265	
266	Commits: [[3]]
267	
268	<intro paragraph...>
269	```
270	
271	`Commits:` uses the file-local `[N]` reference machinery (see
272	[notes/README.md](notes/README.md#reference-numbering)),
273	**double-bracketed** so the brackets render — `Commits: [[3]]`,
274	or `Commits: [[3]],[[5]]` for several. (`[[3]]` shows as a
275	literal `[`, the `[3]` link, then a literal `]`; the inner
276	`[3]` resolves against its `[3]:` definition — CommonMark /
277	GitHub / VS Code all do this.) The `# References` definition
278	puts the **commit URL** as the destination, with the **full
279	40-hex SHA** in the title slot:
280	
281	```
282	[3]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/<12-hex> "<40-hex>"
283	```
284	
285	- The 12-hex short SHA in the URL keeps it short; GitHub /
286	  GitLab resolve a unique prefix to the canonical commit page
287	  (GitLab's path has a `/-/` before `commit/`).
288	- The full SHA in the title is host-agnostic and unambiguous —
289	  it survives a repo host change, `git show <40-hex>` works in
290	  any clone, and external tooling scraping the notes (a
291	  database, say) gets the canonical identifier.
292	
293	**Timing.** The commit doesn't exist when its chores section is
294	written, so the `Commits:` line is **backfilled when the next
295	change to that repo is started** — the cycle-start step grabs
296	the just-pushed commit's URL + SHA and fills it in. The single
297	newest section is briefly `Commits:`-less; that's fine — the
298	commit itself is the record, and `git log --grep "(X.Y.Z)"`
299	finds it.
300	
301	## Cycle Protocol
302	
303	Every change runs as a **cycle**: Preparation → Work-N →
304	Close-out. One protocol; push and squash are actions you take
305	when wanted, not modes chosen up front. The mechanics below
306	run in the order they happen.
307	
308	### Numbering
309	
310	The version suffix on each commit carries its phase, with one
311	separator — `.` — at every depth (semver mandates `-` once at
312	the start of the prerelease, then `.` for each level after):
313	
314	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
315	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
316	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
317	
318	A **trailing `0` identifier marks a Preparation**, and the
319	rule nests to any depth: when a Work commit needs subdividing
320	into its own sub-cycle, append another level — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
321	(Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle), `X.Y.Z-3.1` /
322	`X.Y.Z-3.2` (its Work), `X.Y.Z-3` (its Close-out),
323	`X.Y.Z-3.1.0` (Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle), and
324	so on. A sub-cycle needing no Preparation just omits the `.0`
325	(`-3.1`, `-3.2`, `-3`); one that grows a Preparation later
326	adds `-3.0` with no renumbering of siblings.
327	
328	Each commit carries its phase version in its title **and**
329	`Cargo.toml` — bump `Cargo.toml` at the start of each phase,
330	so `vc-x1 -V` shows the active phase at build time.
331	
332	**Ordering caveat.** The one ordering guarantee semver gives
333	is that the bare `X.Y.Z` close-out outranks every `X.Y.Z-…`
334	dev version. Within the ladder it does *not* hold for nested
335	close-outs — `X.Y.Z-3` sorts *before* its children
336	`X.Y.Z-3.0` / `X.Y.Z-3.1`, because a prefix always precedes
337	its extensions. Cosmetic — nothing depends on within-ladder
338	order — but don't claim monotonicity.
339	
340	**Why numeric suffixes** (`-0`, `-1`, …) rather than `-devN`:
341	semver pre-release identifiers compare per spec and Cargo
342	accepts them. `-dev` adds no information the git log doesn't
343	carry and doubles typing per commit. The final close-out
344	commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) signals completion rather than amending
345	prior commits — history stays readable, exploratory vs final
346	commits obvious.
347	
348	### Preparation
349	
350	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`):
351	
352	- **Backfill the previous cycle's chores `Commits:` ref.**
353	  Grab the just-pushed commit's URL + full SHA, fill in the
354	  newest chores section that was left `Commits:`-less. See
355	  [Chores commit references](#chores-commit-references).
356	- **Bump the version** in `Cargo.toml` to `X.Y.Z-0`.
357	- **Pick up a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one). Move
358	  that entry's text out of `## Todo` and into `## In
359	  Progress` — a **bold title line** (exact match for the
360	  chores section header, minus the `## ` prefix) +
361	  **succinct problem statement** + **plan ladder**. See
362	  [Prose form](#prose-form) (Problem + plan shape). A cycle
363	  that merges several entangled `## Todo` items moves all
364	  of them. A `## Todo` entry that duplicates current
365	  `## In Progress` work is a process bug.
366	- **Open the chores section.** Header = the cycle's
367	  **anticipated close-out title** (e.g. `## refactor: foo
368	  bar (X.Y.Z)`). No body — the cycle's live narrative
369	  lives in `## In Progress` and moves into the section at
370	  close-out per the "title only holds position" rule. One
371	  chores section per cycle is the default; per-commit
372	  sections (like 0.55.0's four) are a deliberate choice.
373	
374	### Per-commit flow
375	
376	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
377	through:
378	
379	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
380	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`, before any code/doc
381	   work.
382	2. **Do the work.**
383	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
384	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit, so
385	   the commit captures the completed state.
386	4. **Cargo cycle** (skip-able for purely-docs commits; full
387	   cycle mandatory at close-out):
388	   1. `cargo fmt`
389	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
390	   3. `cargo test`
391	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
392	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
393	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
394	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
395	   working-copy changes in their editor. Most change
396	   requests come here — iterate until the work is right.
397	6. **Write the commit description.** [Conventional
398	   Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/) with a
399	   version suffix:
400	
401	   ```
402	   <type>: <short description> (<version>)
403	   ```
404	
405	   - **Title**: ≤50 chars. Common types: `feat`, `fix`,
406	     `refactor`, `test`, `docs`, `chore`. The version
407	     suffix counts toward the 50 — favor terse phrasings.
408	   - **Body**: [Prose form](#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
409	     wrap ≤72. App-repo bullets are **file-by-file** — one
410	     bullet per file changed, file plus a one-line gist.
411	     When a file has several distinct changes, list them
412	     as `-` sub-bullets under the file bullet.
413	
414	     ```
415	     - path/to/file1
416	       - first distinct change
417	       - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
418	          with continuation indented 5
419	     ```
420	
421	     The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
422	     cycle's mechanical change record; chores carries the
423	     narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
424	     "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###`
425	     subsection.
426	   - **`ochid:` trailer** as the last line of the body —
427	     see [ochid trailers](#ochid-trailers).
428	   - **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: same shape; bullets
429	     describe in-session activity rather than code changes.
430	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
431	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
432	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
433	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
434	   keeps the verb visible):
435	
436	   ```
437	   jj commit -m \
438	   "<type>: <short description> (<version>)" \
439	   -m "<intro paragraph>
440	
441	   - file1: gist
442	   - file2: gist
443	
444	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
445	   -R .
446	   ```
447	
448	**Two overrides apply:**
449	
450	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
451	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
452	  surface it; don't push through.
453	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
454	  a review or question forward.
455	
456	### Reviewing changes
457	
458	Work review looks at **uncommitted working-copy changes**
459	in your editor, on the way to commit. Zed shows the diff
460	naturally; for terminal use:
461	
462	```
463	jj diff                          # working-copy diff (uncommitted)
464	jj diff -r @-                    # diff of the previous commit
465	jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>      # any two revisions
466	jj show -r <X>                   # description + diff for one rev
467	```
468	
469	Pipe through `delta` or `diff-so-fancy | less -R` for color
470	and side-by-side. To launch your editor as the diff tool:
471	
472	```
473	# ~/.config/jj/config.toml
474	[ui]
475	diff-editor = ["zed", "--diff", "$left", "$right"]
476	```
477	
478	Then `jj diff --from X --to Y` opens it. Don't `jj edit
479	-r @-` to view a past commit — that marks it mutable and
480	shifts the working-copy pointer; use `jj diff -r @-` or
481	`jj show -r @-`.
482	
483	[`notes/substep-protocol.md`](notes/substep-protocol.md) is
484	a longer-form companion (close-out squash recipe, recovery)
485	that predates this rewrite and still uses the old "sub-step"
486	vocabulary — reconciling it is a follow-up. Revset
487	primitives (`@`, `@-`, `..`, `::`, prefix matching) are in
488	[`notes/jj-revsets.md`](notes/jj-revsets.md).
489	
490	### Close-out
491	
492	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`):
493	
494	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
495	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
496	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the chores
497	  section.** Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
498	  `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the chores
499	  `##` header carries the same string) and paste them
500	  under the bare chores `##` header (the title-only
501	  placeholder from Preparation). Sync the chores header to
502	  the **final** commit title if the cycle's scope shifted,
503	  and update every markdown anchor back-reference to it.
504	  Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
505	  commits; `### Outcome` notes if useful. Replace
506	  `## In Progress` with `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
507	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
508	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
509	
510	The close-out commit's body should describe **only what's
511	in that commit** (the bookkeeping: clearing `## In
512	Progress`, the `## Done` entry, chores section finalize)
513	— not what would happen post-squash. Squash / merge /
514	keep is a separate push-time decision (Post close-out
515	review, see [Pushing](#pushing) below); keeping the
516	close-out body shape-neutral avoids locking in
517	squash-language before the decision is made.
518	
519	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
520	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
521	see [Pushing](#pushing).
522	
523	### Pushing
524	
525	**Push is discretionary** — done when you want to back up
526	work or publish interim progress, and **always at
527	close-out** (the cycle's result must be published). Interim
528	pushes are a judgment call; nothing forces push-per-commit.
529	
530	**Post close-out review (close-out push only).** After
531	the close-out commit and before any `jj squash` / `jj
532	rebase` / `jj git push` invocation, stop and ask the user
533	which shape the cycle lands as (squash / merge / keep)
534	and to approve the push. The choice changes the commits
535	on the target and is hard to undo cleanly once pushed;
536	treat it like the Work and Commit Description reviews —
537	surface the options, wait for the user, don't auto-pick.
538	ESC-ESC and the deviation override apply as usual.
539	
540	**Squash, merge, or keep at the close-out push.** Before
541	the publishing push, decide what shape the cycle lands as:
542	
543	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target;
544	  per-commit granularity preserved only in the commit
545	  body's edit list. Right when the work is one logical
546	  change with intermediate validation points. Cost: the
547	  per-commit chores sections collapse into one (its header
548	  becomes the close-out commit title), and every anchor
549	  back-ref to a now-gone section gets re-pointed — real
550	  work, done as part of the squash.
551	- **Merge (non-FF)** — `main` gains one merge commit
552	  (`X.Y.Z`) with parents `<prev>` and the cycle tip; the
553	  sub-step commits stay reachable as the merge's second
554	  parent. `git log` shows everything; `git log
555	  --first-parent` shows main as one jump per cycle. Best
556	  when the sub-steps are worth preserving but main should
557	  read linearly. The paired `.claude` commit's body
558	  carries a multi-line ochid listing every app commit in
559	  the push — the multi-ochid case
560	  [`notes/forks-multi-user.md`](notes/forks-multi-user.md)
561	  designs. Set up with `jj rebase -r <tip> -d <prev> -d
562	  <sub-tip>` before invoking `jj git push`.
563	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on main —
564	  when the decomposition is itself informative and you
565	  want `git log` to show every step. Each chores section
566	  keeps its own header / `Commits:` ref; no consolidation
567	  churn.
568	
569	If squashing or merging, do it before invoking `vc-x1
570	push` (or push manually with `jj git push` for
571	non-standard shapes).
572	
573	The flow is wrapped by `vc-x1 push <bookmark>`, which runs:
574	
575	1. Preflight (`vc-x1 sync --check` + `cargo fmt` / `clippy`
576	   / `test`).
577	2. **Work-review prompt** — shows `jj diff --stat` for
578	   both repos and asks `[y/N]`.
579	3. **Commit-description-review prompt** — uses `--title`
580	   / `--body` or opens `$EDITOR`.
581	4. Commits both repos with ochid trailers, advances the
582	   bookmark, `jj git push --bookmark <bookmark>` for the
583	   app, finalizes `.claude` (its single per-push commit).
584	
585	`<bookmark>` is the **app-side working branch** (often
586	`main`, sometimes a feature branch). `.claude` always pushes
587	its `main` regardless of the app-side bookmark. See
588	`vc-x1 push --help` for the full flag list, resumability
589	(`--from`, `--restart`, `--status`), and recovery; CLAUDE.md
590	keeps only the rules that govern bot behavior. `vc-x1 sync
591	--check` may also be run manually before editing to surface
592	remote changes early.
593	
594	**`.claude` cadence — once per push.** The `.claude`
595	working-copy node accumulates session data across the
596	cycle's commits and is described + committed when a push
597	happens — one push, one `.claude` commit, paired with every
598	code commit in that push. Its change ID is stable from the
599	first code commit that references it through to that
600	`.claude` commit (working-copy snapshots, `jj describe`, and
601	the finalize `jj commit` all preserve the change ID), so the
602	`ochid:` trailers resolve across the push.
603	
604	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives on
605	`main`. The repo has no need for parallel feature-branch
606	bookmarks to mirror app-side branches; when the app sits on
607	a feature branch, `.claude` still commits to `main`.
608	Cross-references between sides are carried by the `ochid:`
609	trailer + commit timestamps; that is enough. **Do not
610	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror app-side
611	branches** — tried during 0.41.1-6.7, went unused, and
612	risks the bot steering session pushes to the wrong remote
613	ref.
614	
615	**Bot communication at the reviews.** Use plain prose —
616	no insider jargon ("Gate N signal", "Checkpoint N", etc.):
617	
618	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
619	  "Work complete. Please review."
620	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE` and
621	  `$BODY` explicitly, then ask permission to commit (or
622	  push). Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push <bookmark>
623	  --yes --title "$TITLE" --body "$BODY"` invocation by
624	  default; it's mechanical and obvious from title/body.
625	  Show it only on explicit request.
626	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
627	  options (squash / merge / keep) with a brief trade-off
628	  summary, plus the push target; wait for the user's
629	  choice before any `jj squash` / `jj rebase` / `jj git
```

```
1	# Substep protocol
2	
3	When a single multi-step `X.Y.Z-N` cycle covers several
4	separable concerns and the per-step diff would be too large
5	to review in one pass, work each concern as its own substep
6	on its own jj `@`. The ladder collapses into a single
7	commit at close-out and ships through the normal
8	`vc-x1 push` flow.
9	
10	This file is the protocol of record. CLAUDE.md points here.
11	
12	## When to use
13	
14	Use substeps when an `X.Y.Z-N` step splits naturally into
15	two or more units of work that:
16	
17	- can be reviewed independently,
18	- want bisectable history during the cycle (so a regression
19	  surfaced by tests can be pinned to one substep),
20	- benefit from intermediate-test gates rather than one
21	  end-of-step gate.
22	
23	Don't use substeps for trivial or single-concern steps. The
24	per-substep `cargo test --bins` cost is real; pay it only
25	when the review and bisection wins are real.
26	
27	## Per-substep contract
28	
29	For each substep:
30	
31	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
32	2. Do the substep's work on this `@`.
33	3. Run `cargo test --bins`. **Non-negotiable.** Build and
34	   clippy alone are not enough — past experience
35	   (0.41.1-6.5) had a regression introduced in an early
36	   substep that didn't surface until a later substep ran
37	   the full suite, by which point bisection cost was
38	   higher than it needed to be.
39	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — give the substep
40	   a working title and body. The title does **not** need a
41	   version suffix during the ladder; close-out collects
42	   every substep into one final commit which carries the
43	   `(X.Y.Z-N)` marker. Substep titles are scratch — they
44	   never reach the remote.
45	
46	The ladder is local-only. Substep commits exist for review
47	and bisection during the cycle and disappear with the
48	close-out squash.
49	
50	## Navigating the ladder
51	
52	For revset basics (chid, cid, `@`, `@-`, `@+`, `..`, `::`,
53	prefix matching) see [`jj-revsets.md`](jj-revsets.md).
54	
55	Common moves while a ladder is active:
56	
57	- `jj log -r 'all()' -R .` — see the whole ladder at a
58	  glance
59	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any commit in
60	  the ladder by chid prefix. Useful for bisection: re-run
61	  the failing test at each candidate without manual
62	  checkout juggling.
63	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two substeps.
64	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one substep's diff in
65	  isolation.
66	
67	### Watch the set of substep commits
68	
69	Below is an example of using a revset using the `::` operator
70	and chosing last commit pushed to the remote, lulqxovr. So
71	by using log and `lu::` we can immediately see all of the commits
72	associated with the substep:
73	```
74	wink@3900x 26-05-01T23:32:51.723Z:~/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1 (@xqpnxtyu)
75	$ jj log -r lu::
76	○  pynknoqu wink@saville.com 2026-05-01 16:16:23 33612c81
77	│  docs: CLAUDE.md substep-protocol pointer
78	@  xqpnxtyu wink@saville.com 2026-05-01 16:16:23 f40c49a7
79	│  docs: substep-protocol.md formal protocol
80	○  lzqmlpvy wink@saville.com 2026-05-01 16:16:23 8c722f8c
81	│  docs: jj-revsets.md review fixes
82	○  wmmxkuoz wink@saville.com 2026-05-01 11:28:46 substep-protocol-x1 65a6705f
83	│  chore: scratch — jj-revsets learning + substep test scaffolding
84	◆  lulqxovr wink@saville.com 2026-04-27 22:27:23 main 55eadc8e
85	│  docs: capture 0.41.1 init+clone redesign plan (0.42.0-4)
86	~
87	```
88	
89	Substep work happens on whatever `@` is currently selected.
90	Use `jj edit -r <chid>` to switch to whichever substep you
91	want to work on — for example, `jj edit -r x` lands on the
92	substep whose chid starts with `x`, where it can be examined,
93	tested, or modified. Modifications to a selected substep
94	rewrite that commit in place, and descendants auto-rebase.
95	
96	## Close-out/squash the substeps into a Step
97	
98	When all substeps are done and `cargo test --bins` is green
99	on the latest:
100	
101	```
102	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
103	```
104	
105	Where `<base>` is the parent of the **first** substep —
106	typically the bookmark or commit the ladder branched from.
107	The `-u` flag (use destination message) tells jj to keep
108	`@`'s description and discard the source descriptions; the
109	intermediate titles served their purpose, and `vc-x1 push`
110	will overwrite `@`'s description anyway.
111	
112	After squash:
113	
114	- All substep diffs are folded into `@`.
115	- The intermediate substep commits become empty and are
116	  abandoned automatically.
117	- History is linear: `<base> → @`.
118	
119	Then run `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` exactly as for any other
120	step. Push's `commit-app` stage finalizes `@` with the
121	close-out title/body and the proper `(X.Y.Z-N)` version
122	suffix.
123	
124	### Validation status
125	
126	The recipe was validated 2026-05-01 against scratch ladders
127	built by [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh):
128	
129	- 3-substep ladder (`base..@-` = {s1, s2}, `@ = s3`):
130	  squash produces `base → @` linear; intermediate commits
131	  abandoned; `@` retains its description.
132	- 5-substep ladder (`base..@-` = {s1..s4}, `@ = s5`): same
133	  outcome at scale.
134	
135	The recipe is N-agnostic for N ≥ 2 substeps. For N = 1 the
136	close-out squash is a no-op (`base..@-` is empty when `@-`
137	is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
138	
139	## Recovery
140	
141	If a substep goes wrong and you want to back out without
142	losing prior substeps:
143	
144	- **Discard the current substep's work.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
145	  drops it and gives you a fresh empty `@` on the same
146	  parent. Earlier substeps are untouched.
147	- **Edit an earlier substep.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
148	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-substep-chid>`
149	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
150	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows the
151	  op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts the repo
152	  to that point. This is a full undo — it removes *all*
153	  substep work after the chosen op. Use only when you want
154	  to start over. **Not** a close-out recipe; the squash
155	  above is.
156	
157	## Worked example
158	
159	A ladder of 3 substeps starting from bookmark `main` at
160	commit `abc1234`:
161	
162	```
163	jj new -R .                                # substep 1
164	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
165	jj describe -m "substep 1: ..." -R .
166	
167	jj new -R .                                # substep 2
168	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
169	jj describe -m "substep 2: ..." -R .
170	
171	jj new -R .                                # substep 3
172	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
173	jj describe -m "substep 3: ..." -R .
174	```
175	
176	`jj log -r 'all()'` now shows:
177	
178	```
179	@  yyy ... substep 3: ...
180	○  xxx ... substep 2: ...
181	○  www ... substep 1: ...
182	○  abc1234 main | <previous step's commit>
183	```
184	
185	Close out:
186	
187	```
188	jj squash --from "main..@-" --into @ -u -R .
189	```
190	
191	Result:
192	
193	```
194	@  yyy ... substep 3: ...   (now contains all substep diffs)
195	○  abc1234 main | <previous step's commit>
196	```
197	
198	Then `vc-x1 push main --title "..." --body "..."` finalizes
199	the cycle.
200	
201	**Note:** Above you see `-R .` being used to explicitly select
202	the current repo. For the bot the explicitness is desirable;
203	for others it's optional.
204	
205	# References
206	
207	- [`jj-revsets.md`](jj-revsets.md) — revset primitives
208	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
209	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
210	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
211	  for squash-recipe experiments.
212	- 0.41.1-6.5 cycle — first multi-substep usage. The
213	  per-substep `cargo test --bins` gate originated there
214	  after a regression introduced in an early substep wasn't
215	  caught until a later substep ran the full suite.
216	
```

Proposed 0.59.0 ladder (broadened — supersedes the original `-1`):

- **`0.59.0-1` Relocate.** Create `notes/cycle-protocol.md` by extracting `## Cycle Protocol` from CLAUDE.md (verbatim, no content changes). Fold substep-protocol.md's still-useful recipes (squash close-out, recovery, sub-cycle navigation) into the new file; delete substep-protocol.md. CLAUDE.md replaces `## Cycle Protocol` with a pointer + tiny essentials (phases, numbering one-liner, link).
- **`0.59.0-2` Refine.** Add `## Ideas` to notes/todo.md between In Progress and Priorities. Update cycle-protocol.md with the three 0.58.0 follow-ups, now Ideas-aware:
  - Preparation triages `## Ideas` (promote to Todo / fold into the picked cycle / drop) before declaring the plan.
  - Close-out captures unprocessed follow-ups into `## Ideas` (was originally "Todos"; Ideas is the better-fit bucket since close-out items are usually unvetted).
  - Merge becomes the default shape for multi-commit cycles.
  - Meta-note that close-out often surfaces follow-ups.
- **`0.59.0` Close-out.** Standard bookkeeping.

**One open question I have a leaning on:** `## Pre-commit Requirements` in CLAUDE.md (chores commit refs, commit-recording headings, markdown anchors) is *consulted by* the Cycle Protocol but is framed as a general writing convention. My take: leave it in CLAUDE.md and link from cycle-protocol.md — moving it widens 0.59.0's blast radius and those rules apply outside the cycle too. Easy to redirect if you'd rather it all live in the new file.

```
630	  push` invocation.
631	
632	**After finalize: stop and wait.** After `vc-x1 finalize`
633	launches — whether mid-session per-push or at session-end —
634	you **MUST NEVER** proceed to a next step, edit files, run
635	tools, or emit any text (prose, recaps, acknowledgements),
636	until the user explicitly directs you to continue. Treat
637	finalize as a hard stop for the whole turn. Any final words
638	must be said in the approval prompt *before* executing
639	finalize; the finalize `Bash` call is the last thing in the
640	turn and nothing follows it.
641	
642	This holds even when the next step seems obvious. Wait. The
643	user controls cadence — every push+finalize is a checkpoint
644	they may want to inspect, think about, hand off, or take a
645	break at. Auto-proceeding bypasses that checkpoint.
646	
647	If push exits before `finalize-claude` (e.g. failure between
648	`push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run finalize by hand:
649	
650	```
651	vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
652	```
653	
654	Clear push's saved state after any out-of-band recovery —
655	`rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push <bookmark>
656	--restart` — otherwise push resumes from a stale stage.
657	
658	A late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded (e.g.
659	updating CLAUDE.md or memory) requires `jj squash
660	--ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a remote rewrite
661	and needs explicit approval like any push.
662	
663	### ochid trailers
664	
665	Every commit body carries `ochid:` trailer(s) linking it to
666	its counterpart(s) in the other repo. The path is
667	workspace-root-relative — `/.claude/<chid>` from the app
668	side, `/<chid>` from the `.claude` side.
669	
670	Because `.claude` commits once per push:
671	
672	- **Code-side commits** each carry one
673	  `ochid: /.claude/<.claude-chid>` — the `.claude`
674	  working-copy change ID, stable until `.claude` is
675	  committed at push time.
676	- **The `.claude` commit** carries one `ochid: /<code-chid>`
677	  per code commit in that push: a single trailer when one
678	  code commit is pushed (the squashed case), a list when
679	  several are. The multi-line `ochid:` list and its
680	  fork / multi-user generalization are designed in
681	  [`notes/forks-multi-user.md`](notes/forks-multi-user.md).
682	
683	Use `vc-x1 chid -s code,bot -L` to capture the change IDs
684	(first line app, second `.claude`).
685	
686	## Code Conventions
687	
688	### Doc comments on every file, function, and method
689	
690	Every `.rs` file must begin with a `//!` module docstring. Every
691	function and method must have a `///` doc comment. Keep them brief —
692	one sentence of purpose is often enough; the discipline is that the
693	comment exists, not that it be long. Doc comments follow the
694	[Prose form](#prose-form) shape (intro + bullets).
695	
696	This is a deliberate override of the generic "write no comments"
697	default that applies to inline `//` comments. Doc comments on the
698	module / item surface are expected; inline explanatory comments
699	inside function bodies remain discouraged unless they capture a
700	non-obvious WHY.
701	
702	**Clap-derive args:** doc comments on `#[arg(...)]` fields drive
703	`--help` output. Clap reflows by default and collapses bullets
704	into running prose. Add `#[arg(verbatim_doc_comment, ...)]` on
705	any field whose doc comment uses bullets so each `- …` lands on
706	its own line in the rendered help.
707	
708	### `// OK: …` comments on `unwrap*` calls (Rust)
709	
710	Non-test code that calls `.unwrap()`, `.unwrap_or(…)`,
711	`.unwrap_or_default()`, or `.unwrap_or_else(…)` must have a trailing
712	`// OK: …` comment that justifies why the call is acceptable.
713	
714	- `// OK: <specific reason>` — document the real precondition,
715	  invariant, or domain reason. Preferred whenever the reason isn't
716	  self-evident.
717	- `// OK: obvious` — the default is self-evident from context (e.g.
718	  `desc.lines().next().unwrap_or("")` — empty desc → empty title).
719	
720	Bare `// OK` is not used (reads like a truncated comment).
721	Abbreviations (e.g. `SE`) are not used because they require a decoder
722	ring for readers seeing the code out of context.
723	
724	For provably-unreachable `.unwrap()` calls, also prefix with
725	`#[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]` so the site stays silent if we enable
726	the project-wide `clippy::unwrap_used` lint later.
727	
728	```rust
729	let max = stderr_level.unwrap_or(LevelFilter::Info); // OK: default verbosity when -v/-vv absent
730	let first_line = desc.lines().next().unwrap_or("");  // OK: obvious
731	
732	match matches.len() {
733	    1 => {
734	        #[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]
735	        // OK: `1 =>` arm guarantees matches.len() == 1
736	        Ok(TitleMatch::One(matches.into_iter().next().unwrap()))
737	    }
738	    // ...
739	}
740	```
741	
742	Tests (`#[cfg(test)]`) are exempt — panicking on setup failure is the
743	correct test behavior.
744	
745	### Ask for clarification on ambiguous input
746	
747	When user input is ambiguous or missing necessary detail, stop and
748	ask a specific question. Do not proceed on a guess and hope the
749	result lands right — a clarifying question costs a few seconds;
750	redoing misaligned work costs much more.
751	
752	### Recognize when stuck
753	
754	If a simple task has eaten 5+ minutes of thinking or back-and-forth
755	without progress, stop. Summarize what's blocking — unclear
756	requirements, unfamiliar API surface, conflicting signals — and ask.
757	Continued flailing produces worse outcomes than a direct "I'm stuck
758	on X."
759	
```

45	`notes/todo.md` is now sized for cheap routine reads — the
46	head limit=60 covers intro + `## In Progress` + `## Priorities`,
47	the live surfaces. The split infrastructure (`notes/bugs.md`,
48	`notes/todo-backlog.md`, re-packed per-file refs) makes
49	future similar splits cheap.
50	
51	## docs: cycle protocol updates (0.59.0)
52	
53	### Squash carve-out (for CLAUDE.md `### Pushing`)
54	
55	Default = merge for any multi-commit cycle. Squash = explicit
56	opt-in for cycles whose sub-steps are intermediate validation
57	points rather than distinct landings. Every cycle is ≥3
58	commits (Preparation + ≥1 Work + Close-out), so the
59	"merge of one parent" edge case is moot.
60	
61	### Close-out follow-up capture (for CLAUDE.md `### Close-out`)
62	
63	At close-out, write any follow-up Todos surfaced during the
64	cycle to `notes/todo.md > ## Todo` (if prioritized) or
65	`notes/todo-backlog.md` (if long-tail). List them in the
66	close-out commit body so the additions are reviewable.
67	
68	### Close-out surfaces follow-ups (meta-note, for `## Cycle Protocol`)
69	
70	Close-out often surfaces follow-up items that became visible
71	only by doing the cycle's work. Capture them at close-out
72	(per the rule above) so they survive `/exit` between cycles
73	— sessions don't carry conversational context across `/exit`.
74	
75	# References
76	
77	[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/a199d062ff6e "a199d062ff6e88b5e2d87d57551d1c60e75b073b"

> DEVELOPER

I'm think this should actually be a new -0 and we should force-push this on the feat/0.59.0 branch we're on, thoughts?

> AGENT

My instinct says **don't force-push**, but the question you're really raising is good — the recorded `-0` describes a narrower cycle than what's actually happening, and the chores-12 design notes are now stale (they say "capture follow-ups to `## Todo`/`## Todo-backlog`" — should be `## Ideas`).

Two reasons against force-push:

1. **Ochid collateral.** The `.claude` commit `af60f979 (0.59.0-0.1)` (already on `.claude/main`) has ochid trailer(s) pointing at the app-side `0.59.0-0` and `0.59.0-0.1` change IDs. Rewriting those app commits invalidates those trailers — the cross-repo links go dangling. Force-pushing `.claude/main` to re-sync them widens the blast radius.
2. **Protocol already handles scope drift.** Close-out re-syncs the chores section title + every anchor back-ref to the final commit title; that's the designed escape valve for cycles whose scope shifted mid-stream. The narrower `-0` isn't "wrong" — it's an honest snapshot of where thinking was at that moment.

What I'd do instead — **`-0.2` "Preparation continuation: broaden scope"** (non-destructive, adds one commit):

- Commits the `## In Progress` block I just rewrote (broadened ladder, Ideas-aware).
- Updates chores-12's `## docs: cycle protocol updates (0.59.0)` design subsections to reflect the new scope: the relocation-to-its-own-file goal, the `## Ideas` design, capture target = `## Ideas` (not Todo/Todo-backlog).
- Maybe a new `### Relocation rationale` subsection to record *why* the scope grew (the "single source of truth" insight).

Then `-1` Relocate implements against an agreed, up-to-date design, and `-2` Refine applies the rule changes cleanly. Three commits added (`-0.2`, `-1`, `-2`) vs two rewritten — same total commit count, but no force-push, no broken ochid links.

The `-0.2` framing is also consistent with CLAUDE.md's numbering rule: "a sub-cycle that grows a Preparation later adds `-3.0` with no renumbering of siblings" — Preparation can extend.

Want me to flip the current `-1 (current)` mark to `-0.2 (current)`, draft the chores-12 design-note updates for review, and proceed that way?

> AGENT

Here's the concrete state:

**Bot-side `af60f979` (already on `main@origin`)** has these trailers:
```
ochid: /vxrtvvrsolplylrryssupszvltpnwrxy   # app-side 0.59.0-0
ochid: /yqzowtqvuwrxznvwnyyoxmwmzrxswlpl   # app-side 0.59.0-0.1
```
The app-side `feat/0.59.0` bookmark currently sits on `0.59.0-0.1` (`yqzowtqv`).

**What close-out squash does:** `jj squash` (or `jj rebase -d main`) collapses `-0`, `-0.1`, `-1`, `-2`, close-out into one new commit on `main` with a **fresh change ID**. The five original chids (`vxrtvvrs`, `yqzowtqv`, and the three not-yet-created ones) become unreachable from `main`. The squash close-out push then creates a new bot-side commit with **one** ochid trailer pointing at the squashed chid.

**The two existing trailers in `af60f979` are not retroactively rewritten by squash** — squash only touches the app side. So those trailers permanently reference chids that exist on:

- **Option K (Keep `feat/0.59.0` on the remote after squash)**: chids resolve everywhere (the bookmark keeps them reachable). Cost: a permanent feature-branch on origin referencing 5 "abandoned relative to main" commits. Trailers stay live for fresh clones forever.
- **Option D (Delete `feat/0.59.0` after squash)**: chids resolve only locally via `jj op log` until GC; fresh clones get dangling refs. Cost: trailers degrade to historical-only.
- **Option F (Force-push `.claude/main` to rewrite `af60f979`)**: replace its two trailers with one pointing at the squashed chid. Cost: another force-push, AND loses the granular per-interim-commit pairing that the existing trailers preserve.

The broadening didn't muck up squash — what changes squash's cost is that **interim work was pushed before close-out** (the `feat/0.59.0` push at end-of-day). That choice was already made; squash now has the K/D/F cleanup decision attached. Merge (the proposed default) sidesteps all of this: no dangling, no remote-cleanup, the bot-side trailers just become first-parent ancestry on `main`.

**My recommendation:** for a *first* squash experiment, **Option K** is the cheapest — squash at close-out, leave `feat/0.59.0` alive on origin as the "long-way" record. `main` reads as one commit per cycle; the trailers in `af60f979` still resolve from any clone; you learn what squash actually feels like; and you can delete `feat/0.59.0` later (or never) without urgency. The narrative ledger in `af60f979`'s body already calls this out ("pushed app side to a feature branch to preserve squash candidacy for close-out") — Option K just commits to that arrangement.

If you'd rather get clean history at the cost of a force-push, Option F is the alternative. Force-pushing `.claude/main` is the same risk profile as force-pushing app-side: low blast radius (private branch on a solo project), but it does rewrite already-published history.

Want to proceed with Option K (continue cycle, squash at close-out, keep `feat/0.59.0`)? Or pivot to merge (no squash, no force-push, no cleanup)? Or do you want to force-push now to redo `-0` and avoid this whole pile of trade-offs?

Another aspect of the discussion is if the repo is a widely used public repo,
like the linux kernel, our current thinking is that bot reops are "never" merged
with other bot repos they are all independent, but they are assumed to be permanent
and public. The receiving project can choose to either "trust" the permanence or
copy the bot repo commits into project controlled repo(s). Our current style is
that the bot repo typically has one commit per cycle and that the history is linear.
Obviously there are no guarantees but that that is the thinking.

I believe, when a change moves/merged from one repo to another, whether it's a code
or bot repo, the chid's are going to change and the commit ochid's must be updated.
Also, if the person cloning the repo isn't using jj the chid's are just random data
with no value as references.

So maybe this technique is BS, thoughts.

> AGENT

Honest answer: **partially BS**, with a clearer scope.

**What actually works** about ochid:

- Within the **original jj-managed repo pair**, using jj clones, no rewrites — chid resolves; you can navigate code↔bot.
- Even for **non-jj readers**, the `/.claude/<...>` *path* is a useful breadcrumb: "session data lives in that directory." The chid is opaque, but the trailer surfaces the dual-repo architecture's existence.
- The **chid-vs-commit-ID choice is doing real work**: chid is stable from working-copy through commit, so the app commit can reference the bot side *before* the bot side commits. Commit IDs only exist after commit, which would force bot-commits-first — breaks the "bot commits last per push" cadence. So if you want a forward reference from app→bot, chid is the only stable handle. That's a real architectural reason for chid, not just jj-fanboyism.

**Where it falls apart** (the parts that smell like BS):

1. **Non-jj readers** — the chid is random data; only the path means anything.
2. **Cross-repo port** — if linux-kernel-style upstream cherry-picks a commit, the ochid trailer points at *the originating project's* bot repo. Still potentially useful as historical provenance ("here's where this came from"), but it's not "the bot session for this commit in this repo." That's a different semantic.
3. **Within-repo squash/abandon** — the chid you reference might not be reachable from any branch anymore. We just learned this concretely.

**The asymmetry you're pointing at** — that app→bot ochid is delicate while bot→app is more solid — is real and worth naming. The **bot commit knows which app commits it pairs with** (it commits last; app SHAs are finalized). The **app commit doesn't yet know its bot pair** (bot hasn't committed yet); it can only reference the working-copy chid as a forward promise.

A more honest design could exploit that asymmetry:

- **App→bot**: drop the chid; just write `session: /.claude/` as a marker. Discoverable, cheap, doesn't pretend a pointer it can't keep.
- **Bot→app**: keep the precise pairing, but use **commit IDs (40-hex SHAs)** — those are universal, jj-agnostic, survive every rewrite that leaves a commit on a branch, and any reader can `git show <sha>` regardless of toolchain.

That would give: app commits read as plain git commits with a "session data exists" hint; bot commits carry the precise, durable, universally-resolvable back-pointer. The cross-project-port case still references the originating repo (unavoidable), but at least the references in their original repo are git-universal SHAs, not jj-only chids.

**Whether this matters right now**: probably not for 0.59.0. The squash question this cycle raises is real, but the fix is the cleanup choice (K/D/F) — that's a one-cycle tactical decision. The ochid redesign is a separate, bigger conversation — worth a backlog Todo so it surfaces when you next find yourself paying the cost.

So: **not throw-out-the-baby BS, but the symmetric pairing is wishful**. Keep the trailers for now; consider redesigning to asymmetric (path-only app→bot, commit-ID bot→app) as a future cycle.

For this cycle's decision: my recommendation hasn't shifted — Option K (squash, keep `feat/0.59.0` as the "long-way" record) gives you a clean `main` and lets the existing ochid trailers continue to resolve via the preserved bookmark. The asymmetric-redesign question becomes a Todo, not a blocker.

The code->bot connection is "most" important, the idea is that we can discover
the conversation that lead to decisions a "ochid" which was "./claude" is useless.
I think what it means is that the tool that merges changes needs to know the
"original" ochids are valid and it has the job of updating them so they remain

Our squash case is the "simplest" example that it is our responsibility to

> AGENT

You're right and that reframes it correctly. The squash case is the canary: if we can't keep ochid valid through *that*, then we can't claim it through merge / fork / port / anything else either. The invariant — "every public ochid resolves in the public graph" — has to be the tool's job.

**Concretely, what that means for squash:**

The squash operation is **symmetric** — both repos squash together:

1. App side: collapse cycle commits into one new commit with chid `X`.
2. Bot side: collapse the bot commits paired with that cycle into one new commit with chid `Y`. Force-push bot main.
3. Squashed app commit's body: `ochid: /.claude/Y`.
4. Squashed bot commit's body: `ochid: /X`.
5. Pre-squash chids on both sides become unreachable from any public bookmark — and that's correct, because the public graph no longer claims them.

That's not "Option F + hand-cleanup." It's *what squash means* under the invariant. The current `vc-x1 push` doesn't do it yet — that's the gap.

**For merge** (the proposed new default):

The non-FF merge preserves the entire cycle's commits as ancestors of `main` via the merge commit's second parent. All pre-existing ochid trailers continue to resolve from any clone. No tool work needed beyond what `vc-x1 push` already does. **Merge is naturally invariant-preserving; squash is not.** That's a real reason to prefer merge as the default — beyond the "linear log reads cleanly" framing in the existing 0.58.0 follow-up.

**For cross-repo port:**

When code moves to a new project, the originating ochid still resolves *in the originating repo*. If the originating bot repo is permanent and public (your stated assumption), "discover the conversation that led to this code" still works — it just happens in the source repo, not the destination. The porting tool should preserve the original trailer as provenance (`imported-from: <originating-repo>/<chid>` or similar) rather than rewrite it. The destination repo is then free to have its own ochid for *its* port commit, pointing at whatever local bot data accompanied the port (if any).

**Practical implication for 0.59.0 and beyond:**

This cycle:
- **Merge** (the proposed default) — no tooling gap, invariant preserved by graph structure. The "first squash candidate" intent for this cycle was set before this conversation; the new info argues for shipping it as merge instead. Cheap. Clean.
- **Manual squash** — workable for this small cycle (one bot commit holds the relevant trailers), but it's a one-off; the next squash needs the same manual labor.

Beyond this cycle — **the real follow-up Todo this conversation surfaces**:

> Build `vc-x1 push --squash` (or `vc-x1 squash`) that performs symmetric squash on both repos: collapses the cycle's app commits into one, collapses the paired bot commits into one, rewrites the two surviving commits' ochid trailers to reference each other, and force-pushes both sides as one atomic operation.

That's the work that decides whether ochid is a real protocol or a polite fiction. Without it, "merge is the only invariant-safe shape" becomes a permanent rule, not a "default."

**My recommendation for 0.59.0:**

1. Ship this cycle as **merge** (consistent with the new default the cycle is itself codifying). No squash gymnastics.
2. Add the symmetric-squash tooling Todo to `notes/todo.md` (or `## Ideas` once we add it). Solid candidate for a near-term cycle since it's load-bearing for the protocol's claims.
3. The 0.59.0 cycle protocol docs should explicitly state the invariant ("ochid trailers must resolve in the public graph") so future shape decisions reference it, and note that merge naturally preserves it while squash requires tooling.

Does that match the framing you were aiming at? If yes, I'll thread "ochid invariant" + "merge preserves it" + "squash needs tooling" into the protocol refinements in `-2`, and add the symmetric-squash Todo.

Got it — same message twice, so let me confirm the position cleanly without repeating myself:

**Agreed**: the ochid invariant ("every public ochid resolves in the public graph") is the **tool's job**, full stop. Any operation that breaks it without restoring it (squash, port, fork, …) makes the technique theater. The squash case is the proof-of-concept for the principle — if we can't make squash maintain the invariant, the trailers everywhere else are just decoration.

What that implies concretely:

- **Merge** doesn't *escape* the principle — it just happens to preserve the invariant by graph structure (second-parent ancestry keeps all cycle chids reachable). No trailer rewriting required because no chid becomes unreachable. So merge is invariant-safe today; it's not a workaround, it's the case where the tool already handles it correctly.
- **Squash** requires the tool to rewrite trailers symmetrically on both repos and force-push. `vc-x1 push` doesn't do this today. Until it does, squash can't safely be used — full stop. Not "use Option K to live with dangling," not "merge as escape hatch." Just: **don't squash until the tool maintains the invariant.**
- **Cross-repo port** requires the porting tool to either rewrite the trailer to a valid destination-repo chid, or replace it with an `imported-from:` provenance trailer pointing at the (permanent, public) originating bot repo. Outside this project's immediate scope, but the same principle.

**For 0.59.0:**
- Close out as **merge** (the proposed new default). Not because it's a fallback — because it's the operation whose invariant `vc-x1 push` already handles correctly.
- Add a `## Todo` (or `## Ideas`) entry: **"build symmetric-squash in `vc-x1 push --squash`"**, with the design sketch (collapse cycle on both repos, rewrite the two surviving commits' ochid trailers to reference each other, force-push both atomically). This is the load-bearing follow-up.

> DEVELOPER

One tweak, the current rule is we never squash bot repo's but we must be able to update the description
so we can update bot->code ochids in it. Here is a key, in a distributed repo, like linux kernel, there
must be a repo that is the one and only "God" repo and it's ochid's are the "truth".

Soooo, when I make a change on a local branch of linux repo those ochids are only valid on my repo.
As my changes migrate across repos those ochids change at every merge. But when they reach the "main"
branch of the "God" repo they must never change again. True/False and is that feasible?

> AGENT

**True**, and yes feasible — under these invariants:

**Why True works:**

- **jj chid stability**: jj's change ID is preserved through `describe`, `edit`, `rebase`, and most operations. Only operations that abandon commits (squash, abandon) change chids. If bot repos never squash (your stated rule), bot chids are stable forever.
- **`jj describe` preserves chid but changes commit ID**: editing a description doesn't touch the chid, just rewrites the git SHA. So "update bot description to fix bot→code trailer" is safe — it doesn't break any code→bot trailer pointing at this bot commit. (Another vote for chid over commit-ID as the trailer payload — `jj describe` would break commit-ID-based trailers.)
- **God main is immutable** (standard public-main rule): once a commit is there, its chid AND description don't change.
- Combine: chids fixed → trailers point at fixed chids → trailer references stay valid forever.

**What the merge tool must do** to honor "ochids change at every merge until God main":

1. **Symmetric squash (local cycle)**: collapse code commits, collapse paired bot commits, set both surviving commits' ochid trailers to reference each other. Force-push both.
2. **Migration to a different repo** (e.g., dev fork → maintainer tree → God main): at each hop, the importing tool must either:
   - **Copy bot commits** from the source bot repo into the destination's bot repo and rewrite the code-side ochid to point at the copies' chids in the destination. Or
   - **Trust** the source bot repo as permanent/public and rewrite the trailer to be repo-qualified (e.g., `ochid: <src-repo-id>/.claude/<chid>`) so it resolves against the source.

   The user's framing — "ochids change at every merge" — means copy-import is the canonical path. The alternative (trust-and-link) is a fallback for projects that don't want to mirror bot data.

3. **At the final God main landing**: the merge tool sets the trailer to point at the chid in God's bot repo (post-copy-import). After this, both the code commit (on God main) and the bot commit (in God bot repo) are immutable; the trailer is permanently valid.

**One subtlety worth flagging:**

When a dev's local cycle has one bot commit referencing N code commits, but only M < N of those code commits actually land in God main (the rest were rejected or merged elsewhere), the imported bot commit's description must be **edited** to drop the dropped code chids and add the new God-side chids. That's a description edit (allowed — chid preserved) on the imported bot commit. The bot commit was *never* in the source God bot repo before this import, so it's not a rewrite of a public commit — it's the initial commit of new content with curated trailers. Clean.

The trickier case is when the import is to an INTERMEDIATE repo (maintainer tree, not God main yet). That intermediate bot repo's commits are *also* immutable once pushed there. But they may not yet match what eventually lands in God main. So intermediate bot repos may carry "tentative" ochids that turn out to need updating when the code finally lands upstream. The resolution: intermediate repos are *also* canonical to their level — what's in the maintainer's bot repo is truth for that maintainer's tree. When the code is forwarded to God main, the merge tool does another copy-import (or trust-link) into God bot repo with God-main-relative trailers. So there's a chain of canonical truths, each level immutable post-publish, with merge-time updates between levels.

**Net answer:**

- True: ochids on God main never change after landing.
- Feasible: the constraints (chid stability, no bot squash, allow bot description edits, copy-import at merge time, immutable mains) compose cleanly.
- Cost: merge-time tooling that knows how to do symmetric updates is real work — `vc-x1 push --squash` is the simplest case, cross-repo import is more involved. But the design is coherent.

The bot-side-no-squash + bot-description-editable rules are doing real load-bearing work in this design. Worth codifying them in cycle-protocol.md alongside the ochid invariant.

301	## Cycle Protocol
302	
303	Every change runs as a **cycle**: Preparation → Work-N →
304	Close-out. One protocol; push and squash are actions you take
305	when wanted, not modes chosen up front. The mechanics below
306	run in the order they happen.
307	
308	### Numbering
309	
310	The version suffix on each commit carries its phase, with one
311	separator — `.` — at every depth (semver mandates `-` once at
312	the start of the prerelease, then `.` for each level after):
313	
314	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
315	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
316	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
317	
318	A **trailing `0` identifier marks a Preparation**, and the
319	rule nests to any depth: when a Work commit needs subdividing
320	into its own sub-cycle, append another level — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
321	(Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle), `X.Y.Z-3.1` /
322	`X.Y.Z-3.2` (its Work), `X.Y.Z-3` (its Close-out),
323	`X.Y.Z-3.1.0` (Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle), and
324	so on. A sub-cycle needing no Preparation just omits the `.0`
325	(`-3.1`, `-3.2`, `-3`); one that grows a Preparation later
326	adds `-3.0` with no renumbering of siblings.
327	
328	Each commit carries its phase version in its title **and**
329	`Cargo.toml` — bump `Cargo.toml` at the start of each phase,
330	so `vc-x1 -V` shows the active phase at build time.
331	
332	**Ordering caveat.** The one ordering guarantee semver gives
333	is that the bare `X.Y.Z` close-out outranks every `X.Y.Z-…`
334	dev version. Within the ladder it does *not* hold for nested
335	close-outs — `X.Y.Z-3` sorts *before* its children
336	`X.Y.Z-3.0` / `X.Y.Z-3.1`, because a prefix always precedes
337	its extensions. Cosmetic — nothing depends on within-ladder
338	order — but don't claim monotonicity.
339	
340	**Why numeric suffixes** (`-0`, `-1`, …) rather than `-devN`:
341	semver pre-release identifiers compare per spec and Cargo
342	accepts them. `-dev` adds no information the git log doesn't
343	carry and doubles typing per commit. The final close-out
344	commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) signals completion rather than amending
345	prior commits — history stays readable, exploratory vs final
346	commits obvious.
347	
348	### Preparation
349	
350	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`):
351	
352	- **Backfill the previous cycle's chores `Commits:` ref.**
353	  Grab the just-pushed commit's URL + full SHA, fill in the
354	  newest chores section that was left `Commits:`-less. See
355	  [Chores commit references](#chores-commit-references).
356	- **Bump the version** in `Cargo.toml` to `X.Y.Z-0`.
357	- **Pick up a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one). Move
358	  that entry's text out of `## Todo` and into `## In
359	  Progress` — a **bold title line** (exact match for the
360	  chores section header, minus the `## ` prefix) +
361	  **succinct problem statement** + **plan ladder**. See
362	  [Prose form](#prose-form) (Problem + plan shape). A cycle
363	  that merges several entangled `## Todo` items moves all
364	  of them. A `## Todo` entry that duplicates current
365	  `## In Progress` work is a process bug.
366	- **Open the chores section.** Header = the cycle's
367	  **anticipated close-out title** (e.g. `## refactor: foo
368	  bar (X.Y.Z)`). No body — the cycle's live narrative
369	  lives in `## In Progress` and moves into the section at
370	  close-out per the "title only holds position" rule. One
371	  chores section per cycle is the default; per-commit
372	  sections (like 0.55.0's four) are a deliberate choice.
373	
374	### Per-commit flow
375	
376	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
377	through:
378	
379	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
380	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`, before any code/doc
381	   work.
382	2. **Do the work.**
383	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
384	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit, so
385	   the commit captures the completed state.
386	4. **Cargo cycle** (skip-able for purely-docs commits; full
387	   cycle mandatory at close-out):
388	   1. `cargo fmt`
389	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
390	   3. `cargo test`
391	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
392	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
393	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
394	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
395	   working-copy changes in their editor. Most change
396	   requests come here — iterate until the work is right.
397	6. **Write the commit description.** [Conventional
398	   Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/) with a
399	   version suffix:
400	
401	   ```
402	   <type>: <short description> (<version>)
403	   ```
404	
405	   - **Title**: ≤50 chars. Common types: `feat`, `fix`,
406	     `refactor`, `test`, `docs`, `chore`. The version
407	     suffix counts toward the 50 — favor terse phrasings.
408	   - **Body**: [Prose form](#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
409	     wrap ≤72. App-repo bullets are **file-by-file** — one
410	     bullet per file changed, file plus a one-line gist.
411	     When a file has several distinct changes, list them
412	     as `-` sub-bullets under the file bullet.
413	
414	     ```
415	     - path/to/file1
416	       - first distinct change
417	       - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
418	          with continuation indented 5
419	     ```
420	
421	     The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
422	     cycle's mechanical change record; chores carries the
423	     narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
424	     "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###`
425	     subsection.
426	   - **`ochid:` trailer** as the last line of the body —
427	     see [ochid trailers](#ochid-trailers).
428	   - **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: same shape; bullets
429	     describe in-session activity rather than code changes.
430	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
431	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
432	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
433	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
434	   keeps the verb visible):
435	
436	   ```
437	   jj commit -m \
438	   "<type>: <short description> (<version>)" \
439	   -m "<intro paragraph>
440	
441	   - file1: gist
442	   - file2: gist
443	
444	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
445	   -R .
446	   ```
447	
448	**Two overrides apply:**
449	
450	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
451	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
452	  surface it; don't push through.
453	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
454	  a review or question forward.
455	
456	### Reviewing changes
457	
458	Work review looks at **uncommitted working-copy changes**
459	in your editor, on the way to commit. Zed shows the diff
460	naturally; for terminal use:
461	
462	```
463	jj diff                          # working-copy diff (uncommitted)
464	jj diff -r @-                    # diff of the previous commit
465	jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>      # any two revisions
466	jj show -r <X>                   # description + diff for one rev
467	```
468	
469	Pipe through `delta` or `diff-so-fancy | less -R` for color
470	and side-by-side. To launch your editor as the diff tool:
471	
472	```
473	# ~/.config/jj/config.toml
474	[ui]
475	diff-editor = ["zed", "--diff", "$left", "$right"]
476	```
477	
478	Then `jj diff --from X --to Y` opens it. Don't `jj edit
479	-r @-` to view a past commit — that marks it mutable and
480	shifts the working-copy pointer; use `jj diff -r @-` or
481	`jj show -r @-`.
482	
483	[`notes/substep-protocol.md`](notes/substep-protocol.md) is
484	a longer-form companion (close-out squash recipe, recovery)
485	that predates this rewrite and still uses the old "sub-step"
486	vocabulary — reconciling it is a follow-up. Revset
487	primitives (`@`, `@-`, `..`, `::`, prefix matching) are in
488	[`notes/jj-revsets.md`](notes/jj-revsets.md).
489	
490	### Close-out
491	
492	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`):
493	
494	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
495	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
496	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the chores
497	  section.** Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
498	  `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the chores
499	  `##` header carries the same string) and paste them
500	  under the bare chores `##` header (the title-only
501	  placeholder from Preparation). Sync the chores header to
502	  the **final** commit title if the cycle's scope shifted,
503	  and update every markdown anchor back-reference to it.
504	  Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
505	  commits; `### Outcome` notes if useful. Replace
506	  `## In Progress` with `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
507	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
508	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
509	
510	The close-out commit's body should describe **only what's
511	in that commit** (the bookkeeping: clearing `## In
512	Progress`, the `## Done` entry, chores section finalize)
513	— not what would happen post-squash. Squash / merge /
514	keep is a separate push-time decision (Post close-out
515	review, see [Pushing](#pushing) below); keeping the
516	close-out body shape-neutral avoids locking in
517	squash-language before the decision is made.
518	
519	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
520	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
521	see [Pushing](#pushing).
522	
523	### Pushing
524	
525	**Push is discretionary** — done when you want to back up
526	work or publish interim progress, and **always at
527	close-out** (the cycle's result must be published). Interim
528	pushes are a judgment call; nothing forces push-per-commit.
529	
530	**Post close-out review (close-out push only).** After
531	the close-out commit and before any `jj squash` / `jj
532	rebase` / `jj git push` invocation, stop and ask the user
533	which shape the cycle lands as (squash / merge / keep)
534	and to approve the push. The choice changes the commits
535	on the target and is hard to undo cleanly once pushed;
536	treat it like the Work and Commit Description reviews —
537	surface the options, wait for the user, don't auto-pick.
538	ESC-ESC and the deviation override apply as usual.
539	
540	**Squash, merge, or keep at the close-out push.** Before
541	the publishing push, decide what shape the cycle lands as:
542	
543	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target;
544	  per-commit granularity preserved only in the commit
545	  body's edit list. Right when the work is one logical
546	  change with intermediate validation points. Cost: the
547	  per-commit chores sections collapse into one (its header
548	  becomes the close-out commit title), and every anchor
549	  back-ref to a now-gone section gets re-pointed — real
550	  work, done as part of the squash.
551	- **Merge (non-FF)** — `main` gains one merge commit
552	  (`X.Y.Z`) with parents `<prev>` and the cycle tip; the
553	  sub-step commits stay reachable as the merge's second
554	  parent. `git log` shows everything; `git log
555	  --first-parent` shows main as one jump per cycle. Best
556	  when the sub-steps are worth preserving but main should
557	  read linearly. The paired `.claude` commit's body
558	  carries a multi-line ochid listing every app commit in
559	  the push — the multi-ochid case
560	  [`notes/forks-multi-user.md`](notes/forks-multi-user.md)
561	  designs. Set up with `jj rebase -r <tip> -d <prev> -d
562	  <sub-tip>` before invoking `jj git push`.
563	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on main —
564	  when the decomposition is itself informative and you
565	  want `git log` to show every step. Each chores section
566	  keeps its own header / `Commits:` ref; no consolidation
567	  churn.
568	
569	If squashing or merging, do it before invoking `vc-x1
570	push` (or push manually with `jj git push` for
571	non-standard shapes).
572	
573	The flow is wrapped by `vc-x1 push <bookmark>`, which runs:
574	
575	1. Preflight (`vc-x1 sync --check` + `cargo fmt` / `clippy`
576	   / `test`).
577	2. **Work-review prompt** — shows `jj diff --stat` for
578	   both repos and asks `[y/N]`.
579	3. **Commit-description-review prompt** — uses `--title`
580	   / `--body` or opens `$EDITOR`.
581	4. Commits both repos with ochid trailers, advances the
582	   bookmark, `jj git push --bookmark <bookmark>` for the
583	   app, finalizes `.claude` (its single per-push commit).
584	
585	`<bookmark>` is the **app-side working branch** (often
586	`main`, sometimes a feature branch). `.claude` always pushes
587	its `main` regardless of the app-side bookmark. See
588	`vc-x1 push --help` for the full flag list, resumability
589	(`--from`, `--restart`, `--status`), and recovery; CLAUDE.md
590	keeps only the rules that govern bot behavior. `vc-x1 sync
591	--check` may also be run manually before editing to surface
592	remote changes early.
593	
594	**`.claude` cadence — once per push.** The `.claude`
595	working-copy node accumulates session data across the
596	cycle's commits and is described + committed when a push
597	happens — one push, one `.claude` commit, paired with every
598	code commit in that push. Its change ID is stable from the
599	first code commit that references it through to that
600	`.claude` commit (working-copy snapshots, `jj describe`, and
601	the finalize `jj commit` all preserve the change ID), so the
602	`ochid:` trailers resolve across the push.
603	
604	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives on
605	`main`. The repo has no need for parallel feature-branch
606	bookmarks to mirror app-side branches; when the app sits on
607	a feature branch, `.claude` still commits to `main`.
608	Cross-references between sides are carried by the `ochid:`
609	trailer + commit timestamps; that is enough. **Do not
610	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror app-side
611	branches** — tried during 0.41.1-6.7, went unused, and
612	risks the bot steering session pushes to the wrong remote
613	ref.
614	
615	**Bot communication at the reviews.** Use plain prose —
616	no insider jargon ("Gate N signal", "Checkpoint N", etc.):
617	
618	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
619	  "Work complete. Please review."
620	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE` and
621	  `$BODY` explicitly, then ask permission to commit (or
622	  push). Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push <bookmark>
623	  --yes --title "$TITLE" --body "$BODY"` invocation by
624	  default; it's mechanical and obvious from title/body.
625	  Show it only on explicit request.
626	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
627	  options (squash / merge / keep) with a brief trade-off
628	  summary, plus the push target; wait for the user's
629	  choice before any `jj squash` / `jj rebase` / `jj git
630	  push` invocation.
631	
632	**After finalize: stop and wait.** After `vc-x1 finalize`
633	launches — whether mid-session per-push or at session-end —
634	you **MUST NEVER** proceed to a next step, edit files, run
635	tools, or emit any text (prose, recaps, acknowledgements),
636	until the user explicitly directs you to continue. Treat
637	finalize as a hard stop for the whole turn. Any final words
638	must be said in the approval prompt *before* executing
639	finalize; the finalize `Bash` call is the last thing in the
640	turn and nothing follows it.
641	
642	This holds even when the next step seems obvious. Wait. The
643	user controls cadence — every push+finalize is a checkpoint
644	they may want to inspect, think about, hand off, or take a
645	break at. Auto-proceeding bypasses that checkpoint.
646	
647	If push exits before `finalize-claude` (e.g. failure between
648	`push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run finalize by hand:
649	
650	```
651	vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
652	```
653	
654	Clear push's saved state after any out-of-band recovery —
655	`rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push <bookmark>
656	--restart` — otherwise push resumes from a stale stage.
657	
658	A late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded (e.g.
659	updating CLAUDE.md or memory) requires `jj squash
660	--ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a remote rewrite
661	and needs explicit approval like any push.
662	
663	### ochid trailers
664	
665	Every commit body carries `ochid:` trailer(s) linking it to
666	its counterpart(s) in the other repo. The path is
667	workspace-root-relative — `/.claude/<chid>` from the app
668	side, `/<chid>` from the `.claude` side.
669	
670	Because `.claude` commits once per push:
671	
672	- **Code-side commits** each carry one
673	  `ochid: /.claude/<.claude-chid>` — the `.claude`
674	  working-copy change ID, stable until `.claude` is
675	  committed at push time.
676	- **The `.claude` commit** carries one `ochid: /<code-chid>`
677	  per code commit in that push: a single trailer when one
678	  code commit is pushed (the squashed case), a list when
679	  several are. The multi-line `ochid:` list and its
680	  fork / multi-user generalization are designed in
681	  [`notes/forks-multi-user.md`](notes/forks-multi-user.md).
682	
683	Use `vc-x1 chid -s code,bot -L` to capture the change IDs
684	(first line app, second `.claude`).
685	
```

```
96	## Close-out/squash the substeps into a Step
97	
98	When all substeps are done and `cargo test --bins` is green
99	on the latest:
100	
101	```
102	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
103	```
104	
105	Where `<base>` is the parent of the **first** substep —
106	typically the bookmark or commit the ladder branched from.
107	The `-u` flag (use destination message) tells jj to keep
108	`@`'s description and discard the source descriptions; the
109	intermediate titles served their purpose, and `vc-x1 push`
110	will overwrite `@`'s description anyway.
111	
112	After squash:
113	
114	- All substep diffs are folded into `@`.
115	- The intermediate substep commits become empty and are
116	  abandoned automatically.
117	- History is linear: `<base> → @`.
118	
119	Then run `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` exactly as for any other
120	step. Push's `commit-app` stage finalizes `@` with the
121	close-out title/body and the proper `(X.Y.Z-N)` version
122	suffix.
123	
124	### Validation status
125	
126	The recipe was validated 2026-05-01 against scratch ladders
127	built by [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh):
128	
129	- 3-substep ladder (`base..@-` = {s1, s2}, `@ = s3`):
130	  squash produces `base → @` linear; intermediate commits
131	  abandoned; `@` retains its description.
132	- 5-substep ladder (`base..@-` = {s1..s4}, `@ = s5`): same
133	  outcome at scale.
134	
135	The recipe is N-agnostic for N ≥ 2 substeps. For N = 1 the
136	close-out squash is a no-op (`base..@-` is empty when `@-`
137	is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
138	
139	## Recovery
140	
141	If a substep goes wrong and you want to back out without
142	losing prior substeps:
143	
144	- **Discard the current substep's work.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
145	  drops it and gives you a fresh empty `@` on the same
146	  parent. Earlier substeps are untouched.
147	- **Edit an earlier substep.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
148	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-substep-chid>`
149	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
150	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows the
151	  op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts the repo
152	  to that point. This is a full undo — it removes *all*
153	  substep work after the chosen op. Use only when you want
154	  to start over. **Not** a close-out recipe; the squash
155	  above is.
156	
157	## Worked example
158	
159	A ladder of 3 substeps starting from bookmark `main` at
160	commit `abc1234`:
161	
162	```
163	jj new -R .                                # substep 1
164	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
165	jj describe -m "substep 1: ..." -R .
166	
167	jj new -R .                                # substep 2
168	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
169	jj describe -m "substep 2: ..." -R .
170	
171	jj new -R .                                # substep 3
172	... edit files, cargo test --bins ...
173	jj describe -m "substep 3: ..." -R .
174	```
175	
176	`jj log -r 'all()'` now shows:
177	
178	```
179	@  yyy ... substep 3: ...
180	○  xxx ... substep 2: ...
181	○  www ... substep 1: ...
182	○  abc1234 main | <previous step's commit>
183	```
184	
185	Close out:
186	
187	```
188	jj squash --from "main..@-" --into @ -u -R .
189	```
190	
191	Result:
192	
193	```
194	@  yyy ... substep 3: ...   (now contains all substep diffs)
195	○  abc1234 main | <previous step's commit>
196	```
197	
198	Then `vc-x1 push main --title "..." --body "..."` finalizes
199	the cycle.
200	
201	**Note:** Above you see `-R .` being used to explicitly select
202	the current repo. For the bot the explicitness is desirable;
203	for others it's optional.
204	
205	# References
206	
207	- [`jj-revsets.md`](jj-revsets.md) — revset primitives
208	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
209	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
210	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
211	  for squash-recipe experiments.
212	- 0.41.1-6.5 cycle — first multi-substep usage. The
213	  per-substep `cargo test --bins` gate originated there
214	  after a regression introduced in an early substep wasn't
215	  caught until a later substep ran the full suite.
216	
```
